**Portugal**,[^cite_note-14] officially the **Portuguese Republic**,[^cite_note-15] is a country in [Southwestern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Europe). [Mainland Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Portugal) is located on the southwestern portion of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula), and [bordered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal%E2%80%93Spain_border) by Spain to the north and east. Portugal also includes the archipelagos of [Madeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira) and the [Azores](3226.md) in the Atlantic Ocean. The country has a population of 11.4 million, and [Lisbon](18091.md), its capital, is the largest city. Portugal's internal waters and territorial sea together account for two-fifths of its territory, and [its exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone_of_Portugal) is one of Europe's largest. The country's terrain contains a diverse range of landscapes and regional climates.

The western Iberian Peninsula has been inhabited since [prehistory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Iberia), with the earliest signs of settlement dating to between 5500 BC and 5300 BC. Muslims had occupied the Iberian Peninsula [since 711](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Guadalete). As a result of the *[Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_in_the_Reconquista)* against them, Portugal was established as a county of the Kingdom of León in 868 and [formally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifestis_Probatum) as a kingdom in 1179. During the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery), the kingdom made several advancements in nautical science and maritime exploration to discover new territories and sea routes, which led to the establishment of the [Portuguese Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire). The kingdom [became a republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5_October_1910_revolution) in 1910, before [becoming a dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/28_May_1926_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in 1926, whose [1974 overthrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnation_Revolution) enabled democracy to be established in 1976.

Portugal is a unitary republic and multi-party representative democracy with four separate [sovereignty organs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereignty_body_\(Portugal\)): president, government, parliament, and judiciary. It has a unicameral national legislature known as the [Assembly of the Republic](1123482.md) (*Assembleia da República*). Portugal is divided into [seven mainland regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NUTS_statistical_regions_of_Portugal) and [two autonomous overseas regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_regions_of_Portugal), though it remains a highly centralised country.

A [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country), Portugal has an advanced economy that chiefly relies upon services, industry, and tourism. Shaped by the various civilisations that have inhabited its territory, such as the Carthaginians and the Romans, Portugal developed a [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Portugal) with a worldwide influence. This allowed Portuguese to become the world's [fifth-most spoken native language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_languages_by_number_of_native_speakers), with more than 250 million native speakers. With a stable foreign policy shaped by its colonial and diplomatic history and location, Portugal is a member of [multiple international organisations and forums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organization_membership_of_Portugal).

## Etymology

The word  *Portugal* derives from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) *[Portus Cale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portus_Cale)*, a [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) name meaning 'port of *Cale*', itself the origin of the name of the Portuguese city of *[Porto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto)*, while *Cale* was a town on the [Douro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douro). There is no consensus on the etymology of *Cale*. It could derive from the [pre-Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Indo-European_languages) word *Kala*, meaning 'shelter' or 'refuge', passed into the [Celtic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Callaecian_language) in the form *Cale*, with the meaning of 'land' or 'mountain'. The [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) *Calaico/a* then came from the [Gallaeci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallaeci), Celtic peoples of northwestern Iberia, and came to mean 'one of the land' or 'one of the place.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalho202276–79-16][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagarinhos2011537–546-17][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESilva2010217–218,_235-18] During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), the region around Portus Cale became known by the [Visigoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigoths) as *Portucale* and by the [Suebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suebi) as *Parochiale*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESilva2010213–262-19] The name *Portucale* evolved into *Portugale*; by the 11th and 12th centuries *Portugal* referred to the region between the Douro and [Minho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minho_\(river\)) rivers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWade202025-20]

## History

Main article: [History of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal)

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistoric Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Iberia)

The region has been inhabited by humans since approximately 400,000 years ago.[^cite_note-22] [Neanderthals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal) roamed the southwestern Iberian Peninsula until 37,000 years ago,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZilhãoCardosoPikeWeninger201193-23][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZilhãod'ErricoBanksTeyssandier202443-24] and [anatomically modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomically_modern_human) arrived in what is now Portugal around 40,000 years ago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShaoKleinWegenerWeniger202515,_18–22-25] The onset of the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Europe) in the area dates to between 5500 BC and 5300 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeyroteo-Stjerna2021265–299-26] In southern Portugal, [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age_Europe) inscribed [stele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stele) have been found, representing early evidence of writing in the Iberian Peninsula.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELuján_Martínez2020561–589-27] Pre-Celtic tribes originally from the [Ukrainian steppes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe_theory) inhabited mainland Portugal and the [Lusitanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusitanians) occupied mostly central, inland regions in 1250 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGorbea2023251–254-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlarcão2001322–324-29] [Celts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celts) may have spread westwards in Iberia in the latter 1st millennium BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESims-Williams2020523-30] [Phoenicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia) colonised Portugal during the 7th and 8th centuries BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrieto2026\[httpsbooksgoogleptbooksid6_qaEQAAQBAJlpgPA18_18\]-31]

### Antiquity and early Middle Ages

Main articles: [Lusitania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusitania), [Gallaecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallaecia), [Hispania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispania), and [Ancient Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Portugal)

Ruins of the Roman Temple of Évora with tall Corinthian columns standing on a raised platform made of rocks in a sunlit town square, set against a bright blue sky, with a medieval tower and white buildings in the background.

The [Roman Temple of Évora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Temple_of_%C3%89vora), built in the 1st century

The Mediterranean coast was well known to the [Carthaginians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthaginian_Iberia). Their close relations with Phoenician-Punic settlements and shared cultural ties likely gave them considerable political and economic influence there from the 6th or 5th century BC, alongside the establishment of unequal treaties with Iberian city-states. After Carthage's defeat by Rome in the [First Punic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Punic_War), it sought new western territories, culminating in the [Barcid conquest of the Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcid_conquest_of_Hispania) in 237 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerrer_AlbeldaPliego_Vázquez202121–22,_30–32-32][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECutillas-VictoriaCaminoAsensio20241-33]

The Romans [invaded the Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Punic_War) in 218 BC and expelled the Carthaginians after the [Battle of Ilipa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ilipa) in 206 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyos2015165–167-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZimmermann2011280–284,_290–291,_293-35] Within 200 years, mainland Portugal had been [annexed by the Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_conquest_of_the_Iberian_Peninsula) despite resistance from local tribes such as the [Lusitanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusitanian_Wars) under the leadership of [Viriathus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viriathus) and other leaders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPires_Pereira2024405–456-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFernández-GötzMaschekRoymans20201633–1635-37][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGil_González2017113–138-38]

In 409, with the decline of the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire), the Iberian Peninsula was invaded by [Germanic tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_tribes).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisney200934–37,_41-39] Western Iberia was integrated into the [Suebian Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suebian_Kingdom), with its capital in or near [Braga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braga).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESánchez2015176–209-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisney200937-41] The Visigoths defeated the Suebi in 585 and ruled the peninsula until the early 8th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELenski2022251–253-42][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins200460–61-43][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastillo_Lozano202332-44]

In 711, the Iberian Peninsula was [invaded from the south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Iberian_Peninsula) by the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate), which expanded rapidly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERusin20258-45] By 716, present-day mainland Portugal was part of the Muslim Iberian territories known as [al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERusin20257–9-46] The Umayyad Caliphate ruled al-Andalus as a province until its independence in 756 under the [Emirate of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba), which in 929 gave way to a [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba_\(Reconquista_state\)) that lasted until it fragmented into states known as [taifas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taifa), collapsing in 1031,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERusin20259–11,_13–15-47] and most of what is now central and southern Portugal falling under the [Taifa of Badajoz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taifa_of_Badajoz).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisney200956-48] The taifas were conquered by Muslim forces from North Africa, first completely by the [Almoravids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravids) by 1115, who were in turn followed by the [Almohads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohads) in the 1140s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisney200957,_64,_78–79-49][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBennison201647,_59–61-50] Northern incursions also occurred during this period, with [Viking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking) raids recorded from 844 into the 11th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPires202292–95-51]

### *Reconquista* and independence

Main articles: [Portugal in the Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_in_the_Reconquista) and [Portugal in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_in_the_Middle_Ages)

Statue of Vímara Peres

[Vímara Peres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%ADmara_Peres)

The *[Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista)* was a series of military and cultural campaigns by northern Iberian Christian polities against al-Andalus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECaraccioli202115-52] The death of the [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_state_of_C%C3%B3rdoba) emir [Abd ar-Rahman II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_ar-Rahman_II) in 852 brought an end to Umayyad efforts to extend authority northward.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELay20097-53] The discovery in the 830s of a tomb believed to be that of [James the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_the_Great) gave the Christians a powerful patron, and the elevation of [Alfonso III of Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_III_of_Asturias) in the 860s ended a period of uncertain leadership, thereby providing the command required to channel this energy into a wave of expansionism southward.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELay20097-53] In 868, the region between the Minho and Douro rivers was secured from the [Moors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moors) by Christian forces under [Vímara Peres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%ADmara_Peres) and constituted as the [County of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_of_Portugal) under him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELay20097-53] The first county of Portugal lasted [until 1071](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Pedroso).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELay200911–13-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGouveia2013176–184-55] In 1096, [Alfonso VI of León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_VI_of_Le%C3%B3n) refounded the county and bestowed it on [Henry of Burgundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry,_Count_of_Portugal), who married Alfonso's [illegitimate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimacy_\(family_law\)) daughter, [Teresa of León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theresa,_Countess_of_Portugal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELay200937–42-56]

Medieval miniature of King Afonso I

King [Afonso I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonso_I_of_Portugal)

After Count Henry's death in 1112, Teresa ruled Portugal as queen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBailey2025170–179-57][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERibeiro_Mirada202566–70-58] Her close association from 1121 with [Fernando Pérez de Traba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_P%C3%A9rez_de_Traba), a Galician noble, displeased the local nobility, who sided with her son, [Afonso Henriques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonso_Henriques), defeating her and her supporters at the [Battle of São Mamede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_S%C3%A3o_Mamede) in 1128.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodoy_da_Costa_Leal_Ferreira202324–37-59][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELay200967-60][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELueiro202314–15-61] The following year, Afonso claimed sole authority over the entire county, free from foreign influence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELay200971–72-62] According to legend, he won the [Battle of Ourique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ourique) and took the title of [King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Portuguese_monarchs) in 1139.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes2024147–172-63][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFranco_da_Costa2014-64][^cite_note-65] Afonso's claim was implicitly recognised by [Alfonso VII of León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_VII_of_Le%C3%B3n) at the [Conference of Zamora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conference_of_Zamora) in 1143, and by [Pope Alexander III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Alexander_III) in 1179 through the [papal bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_bull) *[Manifestis Probatum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifestis_Probatum)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerreira2013-66][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReilly1998\[httpsbooksgoogleptbooksidAVIrEAAAQBAJpgPA80_80-81\]-67][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVeneziani20251–10-68][^cite_note-69]

With the support of north-European [crusaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Crusades) and Christian [military and religious orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_order_\(religious_society\)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECostaLencart20232–6,_8–11-70][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELencart2023662–668-71][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECostaPimenta2009273–284-72] Afonso Henriques and his successors continued pushing south until the [capture of Algarve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_conquest_of_the_Algarve), the southernmost region of mainland Portugal,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECostaPimenta2009281–284-73][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPimenta202053–55-74] which was [recognised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Badajoz_\(1267\)) by the [Kingdom of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Castile) as Portuguese territory in 1267.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2020199–229-75][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendonça19911123–1134-76] With minor readjustments, Portugal's borders have largely remained the same since the signing of the [Treaty of Alcañices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Alca%C3%B1ices) between [Denis of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denis_of_Portugal) and [Fernando IV of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_IV_of_Castile) in 1297.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía2006713–723-77] Between the 14th and early 15th century, Portugal was struck by [several outbreaks of the plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_plague_pandemic), civil wars, invasions, famines, and natural disasters that led to a population decrease, labour shortages, and rise in [antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_antisemitism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDisney2009107–109-78]

### Age of Discovery

Main articles: [Portuguese Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) and [History of Portugal (1415–1578)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal_\(1415%E2%80%931578\))

Painting showing 10 Portuguese vessels near a fortified walled town with a tower rising above the harbour where several vessels lie at anchor, wooded hills stretch into the background, and a distant island appears offshore, with colours shifting from brown-green in the foreground to blue in the distance.

The *Portuguese Carracks Off a Rocky Coast* painting displays Portuguese [vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrack) of the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery) during the 16th century.

After King [Ferdinand I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Portugal)’s death in 1383, his minor daughter [Beatrice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatrice_of_Portugal) became his legal successor under the regency of [Leonor Teles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonor_Teles).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPinto2025158–165-79] Leonor's unpopularity triggered a [crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1383%E2%80%931385_Portuguese_interregnum) compounded by fears that the marriage between Beatrice and [John I of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_I_of_Castile) [could lead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Salvaterra_de_Magos?action=edit\&redlink=1) to the loss of Portugal's independence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPinto2025160-80] In 1385, [John of Aviz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_I_of_Portugal), a [royal bastard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_bastard) and brother of Ferdinand, was [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortes_of_Coimbra_\(1385\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) king and later defeated the Castilians in the [Battle of Aljubarrota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aljubarrota) consolidating the [House of Aviz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Aviz)'s rule over the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEda_Cruz_Coelho2025337–341-81][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteiro2020a63–65-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteiro2020b70–74-83] In 1386, during his reign, Portugal cemented its [alliance with England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Portuguese_Alliance) with the signing of the [Treaty of Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Windsor_\(1386\)), making it the [oldest standing alliance in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_alliances).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilds202424–26,_36–37-84][^cite_note-85] The dynasty contributed to the conditions for Portugal's maritime expansion,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarros_Cardosoda_Silva_Lima2024388–410-86] elevating it in European politics and culture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELima201881–97-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontes2019305–307-88][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESoares2019313–337-89] Portugal acquired its first colonies by [conquering Ceuta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_conquest_of_Ceuta) in [North Africa](21714.md) in 1415.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerrariniMocelim2024518–540-90][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEdos_Santos201725-91][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteiro201763–78-92] Portugal was among the first to start the Age of Discovery,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMukherjee2025357–361"...despite_its_peripheral_position_in_European_affairs,_Portugal_and_not_Spain_kick-started_Europe's_'discovery'_project."-93][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECasimiro20201–17"In_the_Early_Modern_age,_Portugal_was_among_the_first_European_countries_to_engage_in_overseas_trade_and_colonial_ventures."-94][^cite_note-96] and its [scientific advances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_nautical_science) influenced other European countries' overseas expansions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFontes_da_CostaLeitão200935–56-97][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVila-Santa2024332–334-98]

Portugal was the first and the last colonial power in the era of modern European [colonialism](7299.md),[^cite_note-102] and by the mid-16th century it controlled a network of possessions stretching from Lisbon to [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanban_trade) and [Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Timor) through [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Brazil).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVeracini202328-103] Throughout the 15th and 16th centuries, [Portuguese explorers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_exploration_of_Africa#Portuguese) sailed the coast of Africa, established trading posts [for commodities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Portugal#Expansion_of_the_Portuguese_empire_\(15th_and_16th_centuries\)) especially gold and [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Portugal), explored the [Indian Ocean](14580.md) and eastern Asia, and established trade routes in most of southern Asia, taxing most trade criss-crossing the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips201422-104][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmithBoscariol2022609–638-105][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVeracini202320–23-106] However, by the 16th century its enterprise in Asia was already in decline.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuellerAyello_Leite20201,_23-107] The 1494 [Treaty of Tordesillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tordesillas) divided newly encountered non-European territories between Portugal and Spain along a [meridian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_\(geography\)) west of [Cape Verde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Cape_Verde), while the 1529 [Treaty of Zaragoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Zaragoza) extended this partition to the Pacific Ocean.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Grijs2020465–494-108]

In 1498, [Vasco da Gama reached India by sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_discovery_of_the_sea_route_to_India), and two years later [Pedro Álvares Cabral landed in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_of_Brazil) and claimed it for Portugal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMathew2024\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidUBOSEQAAQBAJpgPA110_110\]-109][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVeracini202322-110] During the 15th century, Portugal established the transatlantic slave-trade circuits and, by the mid-19th century, had become one of the longest-active and a significant participant in the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEda_Silva202177–104-111]

### Iberian Union and Restoration

Main articles: [Iberian Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Union) and [History of Portugal (1640–1777)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal_\(1640%E2%80%931777\))

Engraving of the coronation of John IV of Portugal

The coronation of King [John IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_IV_of_Portugal) in Lisbon

The 1578 death of [King Sebastian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian,_King_of_Portugal) in [battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Alc%C3%A1cer_Quibir) and the subsequent 1580 death of [King Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry,_King_of_Portugal) without a named heir, precipitated a [succession crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_succession_crisis_of_1580).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESousa202498–99-112][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArroyo20161–21-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcTaggart2021-114] [Philip II of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain), whose mother was [Isabella of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_of_Portugal), was one of the candidates for the throne.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArroyo20161–21-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrio_Gozalo2025303–305-115] In 1581 he was [acclaimed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortes_of_Tomar?action=edit\&redlink=1) king of Portugal, creating a [personal union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Union) between the two kingdoms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArroyo20161–21-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrio_Gozalo2025303–305-115][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStella1999411–438-116] This deprived Portugal of an independent foreign policy and led to the [Dutch-Portuguese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch-Portuguese_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELousada2024186–193-117][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEda_Costa202129–52-118][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorschberg202448–72-119]

In 1641, [John, Duke of Braganza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_IV_of_Portugal), was proclaimed king by the [Cortes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Cortes) after an uprising led by [some nobles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forty_Conspirators) in the [year before](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_of_the_Independence_\(Portugal\)), ending the Iberian Union under the [House of Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg), and beginning the rule of the [House of Braganza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Braganza).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHespanha199331–32-120][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOliveira2002714–717-121][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETengwall2011448–459-122] Following the [Portuguese Restoration War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Restoration_War), Spain [recognised Afonso VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lisbon_\(1668\)) as king of Portugal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDores_Costa20051–14-123][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitchell2024265-124][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsteves_Soares2025153–155,_174–175-125] During the reign of [John V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_V_of_Portugal), the large influx of Brazilian gold into the royal treasury, chiefly through the [royal fifth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_fifth), produced a [resource curse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_curse) that weakened industry and cereal production, created a [gold rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_rush) from Portugal to Brazil, and led to prolonged economic stagnation, permanent reduction of income growth, and the interruption of the institutional and economic progress achieved in the 17th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKedroskyPalma2025730–766-126]

After the depletion of gold revenues, Portugal was left with a weak industrial base and backward institutional and educational structures. This condition was exacerbated by [Sebastião José de Carvalho e Melo, 1st Marquis of Pombal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A3o_Jos%C3%A9_de_Carvalho_e_Melo,_1st_Marquis_of_Pombal), who emerged as the country's *[de facto](43594.md)* ruler in the aftermath of the [1755 earthquake and tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1755_Lisbon_earthquake), one of the kingdom's worst recorded [natural disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_megathrust_earthquakes). Pombal's industrialisation efforts failed, and his education policies weakened human-capital formation. The [expulsion of the Jesuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suppression_of_the_Society_of_Jesus) reduced student numbers and contributed to Portugal's decline in literacy. Pombal's instrumentalisation of the [Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Inquisition) for political purposes helped to entrench an extractive system that outlasted his rule. The nature of the existing political institutions contributed to Portugal's sustained divergence from Western Europe despite comparable [family values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_values) and high [state capacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capacity), with partial recovery beginning from around the mid-20th century alongside the continuation of colonialism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKedroskyPalma2025730–766-126][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStadheim2023777-127][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESantosRijo2022-128]

### Constitutional monarchy

Main articles: [History of Portugal (1777–1834)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal_\(1777%E2%80%931834\)) and [History of Portugal (1834–1910)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal_\(1834%E2%80%931910\))

&#x20;A large historical painting shows a crowded parliamentary chamber in the middle of a heated debate during the Constituent Cortes of 1820. Dozens of formally dressed men gesture, argue, and rise from their seats beneath towering crystal chandeliers and a richly decorated ceiling, while officials sit at a central desk below a grand canopy and throne.

The [Constituent Cortes of 1820](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Cortes_of_1820) approved the [first constitution of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Constitution_of_1822).

In 1807, Portugal refused [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon)'s demand to join the [Continental System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_System) of embargo against the United Kingdom; [an invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Portugal_\(1807\)) led by [General Junot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Andoche_Junot) followed, and Lisbon was captured in 1807.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJoor201657-129][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeves2016101–104-130] During the [Napoleonic invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_invasions_of_Portugal), the [Portuguese royal family transferred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_court_to_Brazil) the court to [Rio de Janeiro](25936.md), in Brazil, making it the capital between 1808 and 1821.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECavalcanti2007149–199-131] British intervention in the [Peninsular War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_War) helped support Portuguese independence, and all French troops were expelled by 1812.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEErtl2008303-132]

In 1820, an [uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Revolution_of_1820) in [Porto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto) sought to establish a constitution for Portugal, forcing the return of King [John VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_VI_of_Portugal) and his court to mainland Portugal in 1821.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECardoso2022-133] Although the [1822 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Constitution_of_1822) was adopted, the process was marked by compromise and efforts at consensus, which allowed absolutist forces to regain strength, leading to [Miguelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguelist) counter-revolts against liberalism, prompting Prince [Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_I_of_Portugal)'s exile.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECardoso2022-133][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELousada2022287–288-134] After John VI's death in 1826, his eldest son, [Pedro I of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_I_of_Brazil), briefly reigned as Pedro IV of Portugal, granted the [1826 Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Charter_of_1826), and abdicated in favour of his minor daughter, [Maria da Glória](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_da_Gl%C3%B3ria), on the condition that she marry Miguel, who was appointed regent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELousada2022288–290-135] Although Miguel's return was under the condition of swearing allegiance to the Charter, he soon dismantled liberal institutions and was proclaimed king in 1828, leading to the [Liberal Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Wars), after which Pedro forced Miguel's abdication in favour of Maria and his exile causing absolutist ideology to wane.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELousada2022288–290-135]

Under the [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy_\(Portugal\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), the country faced economic crises, political instability, and several coups d'état.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBirmingham2018117–145-136] Portugal's African [colonial expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonisation_of_Africa_\(Portugal\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) culminated in the [1890 British Ultimatum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1890_British_Ultimatum), which thwarted its [ambitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_Map).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatrício201311371–11397-137][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixeira1987687–719-138] The ultimatum and the [1891 Anglo-Portuguese Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Portuguese_Treaty_of_1891), compounded by existing turmoil, led to political erosion and weakened the monarchy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatrício201311397–11408-139][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixeira1987687–719-138]

### First Republic and *Estado Novo*

Main articles: [First Portuguese Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Portuguese_Republic) and [*Estado Novo* (Portugal)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estado_Novo_\(Portugal\))

Black-and-white photograph of José Relvas proclaiming the Republic together with several formally dressed men leaning over the balcony railing of Lisbon City Hall and looking down onto a square surrounded with buildings.

[José Relvas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Relvas) proclaiming the republic on [5 October 1910](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5_October_1910_revolution), at [Lisbon City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon_City_Hall)

In 1908, [King Carlos I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_I_of_Portugal) and [Luís Filipe, Prince Royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_Filipe,_Prince_Royal_of_Portugal), [were assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon_Regicide) by [republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Republican_Party) partisans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorrêa_da_Silva201963–65,_83-140][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarral_Martínez2026123–130-141] In 1910, the monarchy was [replaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5_October_1910_revolution) with a republic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorrêa_da_Silva201984-142][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPires2017331–354-143][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWheeler1972177–193-144] During [World War I](4764461.md), Portugal [fought for the Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_during_World_War_I).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Meneses2025241–246-145] The war increased political instability and deepened the economic crisis creating further chaos and unrest during the [First Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Portuguese_Republic).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERibeiro_de_MenesesAires_Oliveira2023273–292-146][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerraz2023a278–301-147][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadureira2010647–663-148] These conditions led to the [overthrow of the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/28_May_1926_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in 1926 and the establishment of the *[Ditadura Militar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ditadura_Militar_\(Portugal\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerraz2023b3–5,_9–10-149][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiavon2011229–238-150][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELousada2026181–201-151] later the *[Ditadura Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ditadura_Nacional)*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGazalle_Cavichioli20254-152][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreira2020491–493-153] which [evolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1933_Portuguese_constitutional_referendum) into the authoritarian nationalist [corporative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporative) dictatorship of the *[Estado Novo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estado_Novo_\(Portugal\))*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECruz1985347–353-154][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorges_Santos2025838–852-155] under [António de Oliveira Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_de_Oliveira_Salazar).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeal2016128–148-156][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReis_Santos2024400–401,_406–421-157][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerreira_da_Cunha2006187–207-158]

During the *Estado Novo*, Portugal [remained neutral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_during_World_War_II) in [World War II](32927.md), after which the postwar end of colonial legitimacy and the growth of the existing independence movements, rooted in the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression#Effects_by_country), increasingly challenged Portuguese rule overseas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGolson202079–110-159][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchriffl2022474–476-160][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAltinkClayton2027\[httpsbooksgoogleptbooksid3xj_EQAAQBAJpgPT227_201-202,_208-209\]-161] From the 1940s onward, the regime tightened control over colonial affairs and expanded overseas administration, increasing costs and delaying inefficient development programmes. It maintained a fragmented economic and customs empire that favored the [metropole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropole). The regime retained policies intended to preserve the [colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_province) such as forced labour until 1961 and settlement schemes that served metropolitan economic, social and strategic purposes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchriffl2022474–477-162][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAltinkClayton2027\[httpsbooksgoogleptbooksid3xj_EQAAQBAJpgPT230_203-209\]-163] [Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples), already [second-class citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second-class_citizen), saw limited improvements in living conditions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoutinho200087–132-164] This further fuelled nationalist movements,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoutinho200087–132-164][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcia202145–60-165][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECann202191–105-166] ultimately leading to the [Portuguese Colonial War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Colonial_War) from [1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attacks_in_the_north_of_Angola_in_1961?action=edit\&redlink=1) to 1974.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAires_Oliveira2017-167][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJúnior2021121–137-168][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERodrigues2021107–128-169]

### Return to democracy

Main articles: [Carnation Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnation_Revolution) and [Third Portuguese Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Portuguese_Republic)

Black-and-white nighttime photograph of several armoured military vehicles lined up in Rossio Square, with soldiers standing beside them near the Column of Pedro IV and bright neon signs glowing in the background.

Armoured vehicles in Lisbon's [Rossio Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rossio_Square), in 1975

In 1974, a [military coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Carnation_Revolution) overthrew the *Estado Novo* regime,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPimentel2024121–146-170][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrade2024253–254-171] and started the Carnation Revolution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVarelaAlcântara2015151-152-172][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPimentel2024121–146-170][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeite_Pinto2016309–343-173] This initiated the [transition to democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_transition_to_democracy) and the dissolution of Portugal's colonial empire,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECosta_PintoParis20237–18-174][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendesTeixeira2026-175][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELousadaOliveira202515–45-176] from the independence of the [African colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese-speaking_African_countries) to the [Handover of Macau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handover_of_Macau) in 1999.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsgalhado202552–68-177][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacQueen2018172–175,_178-178][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendesSteenhagen2024297–300,_303–306-179] This period was marked by social, military, and political turmoil that culminated in [street politics and violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_Summer_of_1975) and led Portugal to near civil war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPalacios_Cerezales2017688–709-180][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPalacios_Cerezales20031127–1157-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerreira2025223–243-182] The [1975 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_of_25_November_1975) cleared the way for the approval of a new constitution and the holding of elections, beginning to subside tensions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPinto2008305–332-183][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECosta_PintoParis202311,_17–19-184][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERezola2012656–658-185]

After the revolution, in the late 1970s and throughout the 1980s, under the influence of new economic insights, Portugal set about dismantling [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist) policies from that period and moving towards [neoliberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENoronha2022113–128-186] In 1986, Portugal joined the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community) (EEC), which introduced structural reforms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDooley201876–77-187] This led in the 1990s to considerable economic growth driven by [credit-fuelled consumer spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit#Types) and a sharp rise in private indebtedness.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDooley201877–80-188] Together with the growth of the financial and non-tradable sectors, this contributed to macroeconomic imbalances and paved the way for economic stagnation after the 2000s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDooley201880–83-189] It ultimately led to the [2010–2014 Portuguese financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%932014_Portuguese_financial_crisis) resulted in an international bailout and intense [austerity](684037.md) policies, causing lasting impacts such as young adults' employment insecurity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDooley201881–88-190][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPedroso2014-191][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFonsecaLourençoFranciscoCrespo2023322–324-192]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Portugal)

Refer to caption

A [topographic map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topographic_map) of Portugal

Portugal's territory comprises mainland Portugal and the Azores and Madeira archipelagos.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira20204-3] The mainland, commonly referred to as Continental Portugal, is located in the southwest of the Iberian Peninsula in [Southwestern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Europe), while Madeira and the Azores lie in the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira20204-3][^cite_note-193] Portugal's land area is 92,225 km<sup>2</sup> (35,608 sq mi), although [Portuguese law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_law) defines the country's size as 156,597 km<sup>2</sup> (60,462 sq mi) which includes about 64,000 km<sup>2</sup> (24,711 sq mi) of ocean waters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira20204-3][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBessa_Pacheco2014112–116-5][^cite_note-194] [Portugal's exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone_of_Portugal) extends 1,727,408 km<sup>2</sup> (666,956 mi<sup>2</sup>), making it one of the largest in the European Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaptistaNetoBravermanSteinberg2025805–827-195][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECaladoFrazão_SantosQuintelaFonseca2024-196] The country is over two-thirds [wilderness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilderness), almost one-fourth agricultural, and the remainder [human settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_settlement).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Portugal:_forest_profile''20211-197] Portugal's highest point is the summit of [Mount Pico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Pico), located on [Pico Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pico_Island) in the Azores, which rises to an elevation of 2,351 m (7,713 ft) above sea level.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeloNeto_TorresFerreira_FernandesMarques_Lameirinhas2020-198][^cite_note-200]

Portugal can be divided into four morphostructural units:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerasimovMeshcheryakov2006731–733-201] the [Hesperian Massif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesperian_Massif), which occupies most of mainland Portugal; the sedimentary borderlands of the massif, forming the [Lusitanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lusitanian_Basin) and [Algarve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algarve_Basin) basins; the Lower Tagus and Alvalade sedimentary basins; and the volcanic submarine ranges that form the Azores and Madeira.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira20204–5,_13-202] Its [geological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_the_Iberian_Peninsula#Portugal) and [geomorphological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geomorphology) features are largely the product of the [Variscan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variscan), responsible for the formation of the Hesperian Massif, and later the Tethys–Atlantic cycle, responsible for the remaining units.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira20204–13,_29-203]

Regional tectonic processes affected mainland Portugal's morphostructural units differently, producing relief variations that subdivide the territory into ten regional geomorphological units. These units were shaped by the differing regional impacts of [Cenozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenozoic) tectonics, climatic asymmetries in the [Paleogene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleogene), [Neogene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neogene), and [Quaternary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary), and varied lithological responses to tectonic activity and climatic variability.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira202013–29-204]

Continental Portugal is predominantly low-altitude, with over 70% of the territory lying below 400 m (1,300 ft) and less than 12% rising above 700 m (2,300 ft) of elevation. Its geography is structured by the [Tagus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagus) River, which enters from Spain and flows into the [Tagus Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagus_Estuary_Natural_Reserve), as 95% of areas exceeding 400 m (1,300 ft) are situated to the north of the river while the regions south of the Tagus, encompassing [Alentejo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alentejo) and the [Algarve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algarve), have 62% of the lands below 200 m (660 ft). The territory north of the Tagus is marked by mountains and plateaus incised by river valleys, whereas the south is distinguished by rolling plains.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira202013–25-205]

The Madeira archipelago comprises the islands of [Madeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira_Island) and [Porto Santo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_Santo), together with the [Desertas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertas_Islands) and [Savage Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savage_Islands), all of which are of [volcanic origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Madeira). Approximately one-third of Madeira Island lies above 1,000 metres (3,300 ft) in elevation, and its landscape is characterised by a dense network of deep valleys with slopes rising several hundred metres, as well as streams that originate in the island's centre and diverge towards the coast, sustained by abundant rainfall. The steep gradient and very high rainfall is a cause of [flash floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flash_floods) with high [sediment transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sediment_transport).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira202025–26-206]

The Azores is an archipelago composed of nine volcanic islands which, from west to east, are [Flores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flores_Island_\(Azores\)), [Corvo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corvo_Island), [Faial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faial_Island), [Pico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pico_Island), [São Jorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Jorge_Island), [Graciosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graciosa), [Terceira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terceira_Island), [São Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Miguel_Island), and [Santa Maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Maria_Island). The islands retain much of their volcanic landforms, most visibly in volcanic cones and in the lakes that shape the scenery of the islands especially São Miguel, Flores, Terceira, Pico, and Corvo. Although rainfall is abundant, most streams in the Azores are temporary. The small size of the [catchments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin), the [slopes' steepness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stream_gradient), and [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation) accentuates the [torrential regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discharge_regime) of the rivers during periods of intense precipitation causing [erosion problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_erosion) and [floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_disasters_in_the_Azores).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira202026–28-207][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Portugal:_forest_profile''20211-197][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPereiraMendesDias2022-208]

### Climate

Main articles: [Climate of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Portugal) and [Climate change in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Portugal)









The [Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) of Portugal's\
terrain

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeckMcVicarVergopolanBerg20233-209]

The climate of mainland Portugal and Madeira, together with their surrounding waters, is [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate), whereas the Azores and its adjacent waters mainly have a [temperate oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERohliVegaHenderson2024271-210][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdinolfiLoprienoDemoryZeman2025-211][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoraVieira202033–46-212] Portugal's terrain has a diverse variety of regional climates for its size.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoraVieira202033–46-212] The average temperature in Portugal varies from 4–20 °C (39.2–68.0 °F) in the mainland, around 18 °C (64.4 °F) in the Azores, and 9–19 °C (48.2–66.2 °F) in Madeira.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoraVieira202036-213][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERusu20235–15-214][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPinheiro_de_CarvalhoRagoneziOliveiraReis2022-215]

The Iberian Peninsula is located at the southern margin of the temperate zone and at the northern margin of the subtropical [high-pressure zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-pressure_area). Additionally, Portugal's climate is influenced by the seasonal latitudinal shift of the [jet stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_stream), which directly impacts the trajectory of the [polar front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_front). Typically in the winter, the jet stream moves southwards and Portugal comes under the influence of the polar front, producing colder temperatures. When the polar front moves northward, Portugal comes under the influence of the [Azores High](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azores_High) bringing [atmospheric stability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_stability) during the summer.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoraVieira202033–46-212] [Upwelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upwelling) occurs at the coast creating the [Nortada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nortada), a summer wind along the coast.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosPiresSousaTrigo201338–51-216]

Wildfires are a serious challenge in Portugal, with nearly 30% of those [recorded in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Portugal_wildfires) having an undetermined origin and arson accounting for almost five-sixths of the total burned area.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedanoSan-Miguel-AyanzBrogliaDurrant2025102-217] Between 2014 and [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Portugal_wildfires), Portugal recorded an annual average of 12,496 fires and 112,455 ha (434 sq mi) burned, making it the European Union country with both the highest average number of wildfires and the greatest average burned area over that period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedanoSan-Miguel-AyanzBrogliaDurrant202513-218] [Climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Portugal) is projected to raise average temperatures in Portugal by as much as 3–4 °C (37.4–39.2 °F) by 2100 relative to the 1990s–2010s average,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalho2024-219][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhoMeirellesHenriquesNavarro2022353-220][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdinolfiLoprienoDemoryZeman2025-211] with impacts in [water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Portugal),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClaroFonsecaFragaSantos2024-221] [ecosystem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_of_Portugal),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPedersenDiasKokvan_Vuuren2024-222] [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_agriculture),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELimaBentoLemosNogueira2023-223] [health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_human_health),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixeiraCarvalhoRochaPereira2026-224] and [security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_security).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAraújoRamosSoaresMelo20222279–2293-225][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECramerGuiotFaderGarrabou2018972–980-226]

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Biodiversity of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_of_Portugal)

A wide view of a forested mountain valley in the Peneda-Gerês National Park under grey clouds, with rocky slopes on both sides, a stream running through the centre, and a small stone bridge partly hidden among the trees

The [Peneda-Gerês National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peneda-Ger%C3%AAs_National_Park) in [Northern Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Region,_Portugal) is the only national park in Portugal.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECruzSánchezSuárez2024-227]

Portugal is located in the [Mediterranean basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_basin), a [biodiversity hotspot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspot).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESolomou2025147-228] It is home to six terrestrial ecoregions: [Azores temperate mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azores_temperate_mixed_forests), [Cantabrian mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantabrian_mixed_forests), [Madeira evergreen forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira_evergreen_forests), [Iberian sclerophyllous and semi-deciduous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_sclerophyllous_and_semi-deciduous_forests), [Northwest Iberian montane forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Iberian_montane_forests), and [Southwest Iberian Mediterranean sclerophyllous and mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Iberian_Mediterranean_sclerophyllous_and_mixed_forests).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDinersteinOlsonJoshiVynne2017534–545-229] Almost a quarter of its land area is included in the [Natura 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natura_2000) network.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrazão-MoreiraMartins202393–111-230] Native species such as [holm oak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quercus_ilex), [cork oak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cork_oak), [stone pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_pinea), and [maritime pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_pine) make up 72% of the total forested area of continental Portugal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerreiraRibeiroPereira2026-231]

Portugal and Spain have a higher proportion of endemic freshwater fish species than elsewhere in Europe, at 73%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERodelesGaliciaMiranda20162-232] Among Portugal's [protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Portugal) are the [Southwest Alentejo and Vicentine Coast Natural Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Alentejo_and_Vicentine_Coast_Natural_Park), one of Europe's last remaining stretches of wild coastline, and [Montesinho Natural Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montesinho_Natural_Park), which preserves one of the few intact Mediterranean mountain landscapes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBastosDiasBaptistaBatista2012102–103,_113–115-233][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastro2009151–154-234]

Geographical and climatic conditions facilitate the spread of invasive plant species. Over one-fourth of extant plant species in continental Portugal are [exotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introduced_species).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOliveira-Costa2022604,_620–621-235] Portugal has one of the highest numbers of threatened animal and plant species in Europe.[^cite_note-236] Portugal is an important stopover for migratory birds, as its wetlands serve as key wintering and stopover sites for many waders due to their location on the [East Atlantic Flyway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Atlantic_Flyway).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeloDiasJaraAlmeida2023141–149-237] Some mammalian species and subspecies of Portugal such as [red deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_deer), [Iberian ibex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_ibex), [wild boar](52255.md), [red fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox), [Iberian wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_wolf), and [Iberian lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_lynx) were once widespread throughout the country, but intense hunting, habitat degradation, and growing pressure from agriculture and livestock largely reduced their populations in the 19th and 20th centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBencatelSabino-MarquesÁlvaresMoura201970,_72–73,_96,_104,_106,_112,_226–227-238] There are three mammalian species and subspecies, such as the [Portuguese ibex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_ibex) that became extinct, but some mammalian species, such as the [Iberian hare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_hare), have been re-expanding their native range.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESantosCeríaco20253-239][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBencatelSabino-MarquesÁlvaresMoura2019156–157,_226–227-240]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Portugal) and [Politics of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Portugal)

António José Seguro smiling beside a large Portuguese coat of arms.

[António José Seguro](35591042.md)\
[President](516093.md)

Luís Montenegro smiling besides a Portuguese flag.

[Luís Montenegro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_Montenegro)\
[Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Portugal)

Portugal has been a [semi-presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system) representative democratic [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic) since the ratification of the [Constitution of 1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Portugal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Constitution_of_the_Portuguese_Republic''2005Part_IV:_Guaranteeing_and_revision_of_the_Constitution,_Final_and_transitional_provisions,_Article_296,_Clause_2-241][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeto2023122–125,_132-242][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETavares_de_Almeida20101535–1539-243] The Constitution divides the core [structures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_structure) of the Portuguese political system among four [sovereignty organs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereignty_body_\(Portugal\)): the [president](516093.md), the [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Portugal), the [Assembly of the Republic](1123482.md), and the [courts](5108599.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFontes201393–94-244] There is [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) for adults over 18 years of age, with a [secret ballot](479210.md) for all elected offices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETavares_de_Almeida20101535–1537-245] Portugal operates a competitive multi-party system at the national, regional, and local levels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Sousada_CruzFernandes20231–29-246][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERuel202185–91-247][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalcaRuel2024704–718-248] There have been trends towards [autocratisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarinaAltmanFernandesGod202641"The_eight_countries_that_are_very_close_to_becoming_autocratizers_are:_Bulgaria,_Cyprus,_Namibia,_Portugal,_Russia,_Sierra_Leone,_Sudan,_and_Vanuatu._Three_of_the_eight_countries_are_new_on_the_Watchlist_this_year:_Bulgaria,_Portugal,_and_Vanuatu."-249][^cite_note-250]

The [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_of_Portugal) of Portugal is the president who is elected to a five-year term by direct, [universal adult suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_adult_suffrage).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENarciso2023210-251][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendonça202352-252][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETavares_de_Almeida20101536–1537-253] The president is usually regarded as politically independent and carries [moderating powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderating_power_\(Portugal\)) aimed at ensuring the regular functioning of institutions and constitutional guarantees;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraça_Feijó2021164,_166-254][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGomesAlbergariaCanotilho2021478–479,_517-255][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeijó201939–41-256][^cite_note-258] in addition, it includes [supreme command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the armed forces,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGomes_CanotilhoMoreira1991101–106-259] and shapes political opinions and agendas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrimaldi2023196–205,_222–228,_380–396-260][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraça_Feijó202190–95-261][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmorim_NetoAnselmo2024704–721-262] The current president of Portugal is [António José Seguro](35591042.md); he [took office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_presidential_inauguration?action=edit\&redlink=1) after winning the runoff vote of the [2026 Portuguese presidential election](68734153.md).[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

Photograph of Belém Palace. A large pale pink palace with tiled roofs sits behind a walled terrace and the Afonso de Albuquerque Square, with statues, trimmed hedges, and a few cars passing along the street in front.

The [Belém Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bel%C3%A9m_Palace), residence and workplace of the president

Photograph of São Bento Palace. A large white neoclassical government building with tall columns and sculpted pediments rises above a green lawn under a clear blue sky, with the Portuguese flag flying from the roof.

The [São Bento Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Bento_Palace), seat of the [Assembly of the Republic](1123482.md)

Portugal's legislative body is the [Assembly of the Republic](1123482.md), a [unicameral](204304.md) parliament.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoesLeston-Bandeira2022\[httpsbooksgoogleptbooksid_DeKEAAAQBAJpgPA138_138\]-265][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELosada_Maestre2021227-266] It consists of a single chamber with a minimum of 180 seats and a maximum of 230, elected by popular vote every four years or when dissolved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENarciso2023209-267][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoesLeston-Bandeira2022\[httpsbooksgoogleptbooksid_DeKEAAAQBAJpgPA138_138-140\]-268][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeto2023131–132-269] The members of parliament represent the whole country and not the constituencies for which they are elected.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESilvaGiovannetti202269-270] The Assembly of the Republic is dominated by three political parties, the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_\(Portugal\)) (PSD), [Chega](60708659.md) (CH), and the [Socialist Party](403237.md) (PS).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMendes2025469–494-271]

As the head of government, the [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Portugal) directs government policy, leads the [Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_\(Portugal\)) which includes ministers and junior ministers that execute government policy, and is appointed by the president in light of electoral results after consulting with the parties with parliament seats.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBelchiorSilveira2023310–329-272][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESilveiraSilva2023150-273][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeto2023123-274] Portugal's current prime minister is [Luís Montenegro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_Montenegro), who took office after the [AD – PSD/CDS Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Alliance_\(Portugal,_2024\)) won enough seats to form a minority government following the [2024 Portuguese legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Portuguese_legislative_election).[^cite_note-275]

Portugal has a [civil law system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) based on [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law) and on [Canon Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_Law) and influenced by [German civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%BCrgerliches_Gesetzbuch).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamza20122–4-276] The Constitution is the [supreme law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_law) of Portugal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEViolante2023133–136-277] In the Portuguese legal system, [private civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_law) and [criminal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_law) are codified in the *[Código Civil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Civil_Code)* and the *[Código Penal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_Code_of_Portugal)* respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMączyńskiMoura_Vicente20172433-278][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELíbano_Monteiro2024111–118-279] Portugal's court system is organised into judicial, administrative, and fiscal branches with three levels of jurisdiction and separate courts of last appeal, while the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_\(Portugal\)) is responsible for constitutional review and oversight of elections, referendums and political parties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENarciso2023211-280]

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Portugal)

Portugal is a [highly centralised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centralized_government) [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) comprising two autonomous regions, as well as seven [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NUTS_statistical_regions_of_Portugal) on the [mainland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Portugal). Subnational government in Portugal is currently organised through municipalities ([concelhos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concelhos)), civil parishes ([freguesias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freguesias)), and intermunicipal communities ([comunidades intermunicipais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermunicipal_communities_of_Portugal)). In the following table, the intermunicipal communities are grouped by region:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development202011,_66–75-281][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESzabóFarkas2024203–204-282]
