### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Portugal)

Map of international relations of Portugal

[Diplomatic missions of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_missions_of_Portugal)

[^cite_note-283]

&#x20; Countries that host a Portuguese Embassy or Consulate

&#x20; Countries that do not host Portuguese diplomatic missions

&#x20; Portugal

A member state of the [United Nations](31769.md) since 1955,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastaño20159–38-284] Portugal is a [member of almost all major international organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organization_membership_of_Portugal),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPereira2018281-285] and is among the countries that founded [NATO](21133.md) in 1949,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixeira199915–41-286] the [OECD](33853117.md) in 1961,[^cite_note-287] [EFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association) in 1960,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndresen-Leitão2004285–312-288] and the [Community of Portuguese Language Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_of_Portuguese_Language_Countries) (CPLP) in 1996, an international organisation of countries with Portuguese as an official language.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreixo200635–54-289]

Portugal has a stable foreign policy that is the result of its history, geography, and specific foreign policy choices. It is structured around six principal regional and policy priorities: Europe, NATO and relations with the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations) and with the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal%E2%80%93United_States_relations), the [Portuguese-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese-speaking_world), the importance of and support for Portuguese communities abroad, the internationalisation of the [national economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Portugal), and the strengthening of [multilateralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateralism). Portugal regards the success of [European integration](683704.md) as paramount and is prepared, where a consensus exists among European Union member states, to endorse policies that go against with the country's national interests;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPereira2018269–283-290] it also supports general disarmament, the dismantling of political-military blocs, and the creation of a collective security system aimed at establishing an international order grounded in peace and justice among peoples.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoutinhoPiçarra2019629–632-291]

Portugal has two territorial disputes, both of which are with Spain: the Spanish town of [Olivenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivenza) which has been claimed by Portugal since the [19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Oranges), and the surrounding waters of the Portuguese Savage Islands which have been claimed by Spain since the 15th century. Despite causing moments of tension between the two countries, the [relationship between the two countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal%E2%80%93Spain_relations) remains excellent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGómez_Adeva2017752–765-292][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESantos_Sánchez202223–44-293][^cite_note-294]

### Military

Main article: [Portuguese Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Armed_Forces)

Photograph of NRP Bartolomeu Dias (F333) of the Portuguese Navy docked by the quays of Hernesaari, Helsinki, Finland, with the Portuguese flag flying at the stern and calm blue water stretching out to the open sea beyond.

[Portuguese Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Navy) frigate NRP Bartolomeu Dias

The [Portuguese Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Armed_Forces) consist of three branches commanded by the *[Estado-Maior-General das Forças Armadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_General_Staff_\(Portugal\))* (Armed Forces General Staff): the *[Marinha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Navy)* (Navy), *[Exército](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Army)* (Army), and *[Força Aérea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Air_Force)* (Air Force).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarreiras2025185–186-295] In addition to the three branches of the armed forces, there is the *[Guarda Nacional Republicana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Republican_Guard_\(Portugal\))* (National Republican Guard), a [gendarmerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie), comprising 23,042 personnel in 2025, under the authority of both the [Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Defence_\(Portugal\)) and the [Home Affairs](19774688.md) ministries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd-JonesCerezales200790-296][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDepartamento_de_Informação_da_Organização_do_Estado_e_do_Emprego_Público2025a-297] The Portuguese military serves as a self-defence force, takes part in humanitarian and peace missions undertaken by the international organisations to which Portugal belongs, and cooperates in civil defence missions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarreiras2025183–197-298] In recent years, the Portuguese military have carried out several [NATO](21133.md) and [European Union](9317.md) missions worldwide.[^cite_note-299] The [Portuguese military budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_with_highest_military_expenditures) in 2025 was estimated at more than US\$6 billion, representing 2% of GDP.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Defence_expenditure_of_NATO_countries_(2014–2025)''20254,_8-300]

As of 2025, the three branches numbered 23,589 military personnel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDepartamento_de_Informação_da_Organização_do_Estado_e_do_Emprego_Público2025b7-301] Prior to 1999, military service was compulsory for men at age 18. This obligation was suspended in 1999 and was replaced by a mandatory day of education about the Armed Forces called *[Dia da Defesa Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defence_Day_\(Portugal\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (National Defence Day).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEResende200911–24-302] Since 1992 women may serve in all branches of the armed forces. As of 2004, the military is entirely composed of volunteers and professionals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommittee_on_Women_in_the_NATO_Forces20081–3-303]

### Law enforcement

Main articles: [Law of Portugal](4584049.md), [Judiciary of Portugal](5108599.md), [Law enforcement in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Portugal), and [Crime in Portugal](15021761.md)

Photograph of the headquarters of the Judicial Police (PJ). A large modern white-and-grey office building with irregular window patterns stands on a corner under an overcast sky, with parked motorcycles, a few pedestrians, and a crosswalk in the foreground.

The headquarters of the *[Polícia Judiciária](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pol%C3%ADcia_Judici%C3%A1ria)* (Judiciary Police), in Lisbon

In Portugal, public prosecution is conducted by the [Public Prosecution Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Prosecution_Service_\(Portugal\)) which is headed by the [prosecutor general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecutor_General_of_Portugal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENarciso2023208–212-304] The main police organisations of Portugal are the *Guarda Nacional Republicana* (National Republican Guard), the *[Polícia de Segurança Pública](5640844.md)* (Public Security Police), a civilian police force that works in urban areas; and the *[Polícia Judiciária](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pol%C3%ADcia_Judici%C3%A1ria)* (Judiciary Police), a senior criminal police body, under the authority of the [Minister of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(Portugal\)) and endowed with administrative autonomy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd-JonesCerezales200790-296][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERocha_GonçalvesDurão20174-305][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECardoso2020189–193-306]

Portugal has [49 correctional facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_prisons_in_Portugal?action=edit\&redlink=1) run by the [Directorate-General for Reintegration and Prison Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate-General_for_Reintegration_and_Prison_Services?action=edit\&redlink=1) (DGRSP).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECondeSousaPitaSouto2026678,_680-307] The facilities are classified by security level, and by degree of management complexity, and often house inmates with different legal statuses, risk levels, and criminal trajectories within the same facilities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECondeSousaPitaSouto2026678-308] Despite following a reintegration-oriented penal model, Portugal retains high prison occupancy and an average sentence length of 31.3 months and one of the world's highest [prison suicide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoner_suicide) rates representing around 30% of the 60 to 70 [deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_in_custody) occurring annually.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECondeSousaPitaSouto2026678-308][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELança_Pereira2026181–197-309]

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Portugal)

Portugal has a tradition of a humanistic criminal justice.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiranda_RodriguesJoão_AntunesFidalgoHorta_Pinto20211-310] The Portuguese Constitution defines the country as being one that is based on human dignity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartinsRoque2013312–314-311] It abolished [capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Portugal) and [life imprisonment](148681.md) in the 19th century,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiranda_RodriguesJoão_AntunesFidalgoHorta_Pinto20211-310] and forbids [extradition](239648.md) in the case of either sentence possibly being imposed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECostaCaeiro2021281–283,_299–304-312] The [Portuguese Penal Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Penal_Code) provides for a wide range of non-custodial sentences, with the aim of keeping a prison sentence a punishment of last resort.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiranda_RodriguesJoão_AntunesFidalgoHorta_Pinto20211-310] Portuguese penitentiary laws have traditionally been progressive, and based on rehabilitation as the main goal of the implementation of such a sentence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiranda_RodriguesJoão_AntunesFidalgoHorta_Pinto20211-310]

Since the 1980s, Portugal has strengthened [LGBTQ rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Portugal) and become one of [Europe's most equal countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Europe), although [historical isolation and authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_history_in_Portugal) continues to shape social acceptance of [LGBTQ people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_people_in_Portugal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamalho2026203–232-313][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGatoPortugalSoteroSeabra2025146–149,_161-314][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENevesBorgesFerreiraCorreia2025349–364-315] In 2001, Portugal [decriminalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_policy_of_Portugal) the personal possession and consumption of all drugs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERêgoOliveiraLameiraCruz2021-316] Portugal faces issues such as [unwarranted use of force by law enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Visit_report_-_Portugal_-_November_2024''20258–14-317][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECasquilho-MartinsBelchior-RochaAlves20221–19-318] [racism](14128594.md) and discrimination against minorities,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Survey_on_living_conditions,_origins_and_trajectories_of_the_resident_population_2023''2023-319][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELoza202429–37-320] [migrant slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_contemporary_Portugal),[^cite_note-321] restrictions on [freedom of association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_association) and collective bargaining,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_States_Department_of_State_-_Bureau_of_Democracy,_Human_Rights,_and_Labor20253-322] violations of wage, hour, and overtime laws,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_States_Department_of_State_-_Bureau_of_Democracy,_Human_Rights,_and_Labor20255-323] and persistent structural and institutional deficiencies in the prison system.[^cite_note-324]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Portugal)

Photography of Parque das Nações, in Lisbon. Modern waterfront buildings, including two tall glass-and-concrete towers with sail-shaped white roofs, can be seen across the Tagus River under a blue sky with few thin white clouds. Trees, a riverside promenade, pedestrians, and nearby commercial buildings line the waterfront.

[Parque das Nações](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_das_Na%C3%A7%C3%B5es), in Lisbon; an economic centre in Portugal

Portugal is a [high-income country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_country) with an [advanced economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_economy) that follows the [Mediterranean model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_social_model#Mediterranean_model).[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''World_economic_outlook:_global_economy_in_the_shadow_of_war''202699-326][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGentilucci2020181-327] The country's economic policy is framed by strategic guidelines called [Major Options](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_Options?action=edit\&redlink=1), which are subsequently implemented through the [State Budget](4360909.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development202364-328] Portugal has the EU's [14th-largest economy by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_in_Europe_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and the [12th-largest economy by PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_in_Europe_by_GDP_\(PPP\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''World_economic_outlook:_global_economy_in_the_shadow_of_war''202639,_120,_123,_127–128,_137–138-329] Its PPP-adjusted GDP per capita stood at to 81% of the EU average in 2025.[^cite_note-330] As of 2024, Portugal's service sector contributed the most for its economic output followed the industrial sector and its [primary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_sector).[^cite_note-sectorsGDP-331] In 2025, Portugal [unemployment rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_unemployment_rate) was 5.8%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Employment_statistics_–_4th_quarter_of_2025''2026-332] As of 2024, its poverty rate after social transfers was 15.4% of the population while in 2023, the at-risk-of-poverty rate before social transfers stood at 40.3%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Income_and_living_conditions_–_2025''2025-333][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETavaresCarmo202513-334] The [national debt of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_national_debt) was estimated at 89.2% relative to GDP as of 2025.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Excessive_deficit_procedure:_2nd_notification_2026''2026-335]

Portugal is part of the [European single market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_single_market) which represents more than 450 million consumers.[^cite_note-336] Portugal replaced the [escudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_escudo) with the [euro](9472.md) in 2002.[^cite_note-337] Its monetary policy is set by the [European Central Bank](9474.md).[^cite_note-338] The country has been a part of the [Eurozone](184391.md) since its inception.[^cite_note-339] Portugal's [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank) is the [Banco de Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banco_de_Portugal) and it is part of the [European System of Central Banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_System_of_Central_Banks).[^cite_note-340] In 2024, Portugal had a combined share of exports and imports that amounted to 90% of its total GDP.[^cite_note-341]

In February 2026, Portugal's main export markets were Spain at 26% and Germany at 13.9%. Its main exports are machinery and mechanical appliances, vehicles and other transportation equipment, base metals, and plastics. Portugal's main import markets in 2026 were Spain at 32.9% and Germany at 11.9%. Portugal's main imports are machinery and mechanical appliances, chemical products, agricultural products, and mineral fuels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''International_trade_–_March_2026''2026-342]

Since the 1990s, Portugal's economic model has been based on [public consumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumption_\(economics\)) and [economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_development) focused on exports, private [investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investment_\(macroeconomics\)) and the development of its [high-tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-tech) sector. Consequently, business services have overtaken more traditional industries such as [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine) and [cork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cork_\(material\)) in export earnings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Financial_Times_special_report:_investing_in_Portugal''20144-343] Operated by the [Euronext Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euronext_Lisbon), Portugal's stock market, the [PSI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSI-20), includes 16 major companies based on the country, including [Sonae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonae), [Mota-Engil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mota-Engil), [Corticeira Amorim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corticeira_Amorim), [The Navigator Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Navigator_Company), and [EDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EDP_Group).[^cite_note-psi-344]

In 2025, Portugal ranked 16th among European Union member states in innovation by the [European Innovation Scoreboard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Innovation_Scoreboard), while placing 31st worldwide in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEuropean_Commission:_Directorate-General_for_Research_and_Innovation202571-345][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Global_Innovation_Index_2025:_innovation_at_a_crossroads''202568-346] In 2024, neuroscience accounted for 12.5% of Portugal's [scientific output](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Portugal), the second-highest national share, and the country ranked 23rd worldwide in total neuroscience output.[^cite_note-347] Among the research institutions based in the country are the [Gulbenkian Institute of Molecular Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulbenkian_Institute_of_Molecular_Medicine), the [International Iberian Nanotechnology Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Iberian_Nanotechnology_Laboratory), and the [Champalimaud Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champalimaud_Foundation) which was the world's 11th top non-profit institution in neuroscience research between 2019 and 2023.[^cite_note-348]

### Agriculture and fishery

Main articles: [Agriculture in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Portugal) and [Fishing industry in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_industry_in_Portugal)

A long pile of harvested cork bark stacked in a grassy field.

The harvesting of [cork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cork_\(material\)) bark. The majority of the world's cork is produced in Portugal.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERodriguesGevrekMota202319–20-349]

The Portuguese agriculture sector accounted for 2.9% of the country's total GDP as of 2024.[^cite_note-sectorsGDP-331] Portugal's [agricultural land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_land) encompassed an area of approximately 3,697,000 ha (36,970 km<sup>2</sup>) as of 2023, of which 17.4% was dedicated to [organic farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_farming), representing the fifth-highest share worldwide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''World_food_and_agriculture_–_statistical_yearbook_2025''202545,_348,_368-350] Despite the extent of the country's agricultural land, the diversity of Portugal's [edaphoclimatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edaphology) and [agroecological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agroecology) conditions enables the cultivation of various agricultural products such as wheat, maize, and rice, with each one having an important role in the primary sector, and resulting in an agricultural self-sufficiency rate of 85% as of 2018.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVianaFreireAbrantesRocha20212-351][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabinete_de_Planeamento,_Políticas_e_Administração_Geral202175–76-352]

Portugal ranks sixth in the world in [seafood consumption per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_seafood_consumption), with each Portuguese person consuming on average 54 kg (119 lb) of fish in 2023.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Fishery_and_aquaculture_statistics_–_yearbook_2023''202592-353] The high fish consumption in Portugal is due to tradition and cultural roots, politics, dynamics of the fish market system, and geography.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurthyGalliMadeiraMoreno_Pires20232–3-354] Strong demand for seafood makes the fisheries a valuable national asset.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurthyGalliMadeiraMoreno_Pires20233-355] While Portugal captured 185,000 tonnes (182,000 long tons; 204,000 short tons) of fish in 2019, down from the 222,000 t (218,000 long tons; 245,000 short tons) in 2010, fish and seafood represent the single highest import of biocapacity from abroad, amounting to 1,600,000 [global hectares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_hectare) in total.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurthyGalliMadeiraMoreno_Pires20232-356]

### Industry and services

Main articles: [Tourism in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Portugal) and [Automotive industry in Portugal](https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ind%C3%BAstria%20autom%C3%B3vel%20em%20Portugal)

Rows of newly manufactured cars parked at a port in front of a Grimaldi Lines vehicle carrier.

[Volkswagen Autoeuropa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen_Autoeuropa) cars in the Port of Setúbal

Portugal's manufacturing industry accounted for 21.2% of the country's total GDP as of 2024, down from an annual average of 26% over the period between 1953 and 1973.[^cite_note-sectorsGDP-331][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerraz20244-357] The lower contribution of manufacturing to Portugal's economy has led to lower real GDP growth rates between 1974 and 2019 than during the period between 1950 and 1973.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerraz20244-357] As of 2025, Portugal's automobile industry produced the biggest share of the country's exports.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''International_trade_–_March_2026''2026-342] The automobile industry accounts for 20.2% of the country manufacturing exports and 82.8% of the total value of its exports employing 43.247 workers in 2020.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Automotive_industry_in_Portugal''20224-358]

Portugal's service sector accounted for 76.5% of the country's total economic output as of 2024.[^cite_note-sectorsGDP-331] Tourism, retail, and telecommunication are all major industries.[^cite_note-psi-344] Tourism represented 11.8% of the Portugal's total GDP as of 2025.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Tourism_satellite_account_–_2025''2026-359] Portugal attracted nearly 30 million international tourists in 2025,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Tourism_statistics_-_2025''20262-360] [ranking 15th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_rankings) in the world in that year for inbound tourism.[^cite_note-361]

### Infrastructure

Main articles: [Transport in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Portugal) and [Energy in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Portugal)

Alqueva dam with a road across its crest holds back a reservoir in a rural landscape.

The [Alqueva Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alqueva_Dam) is one of the largest strategic water reserves in Europe.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESantosPais_Dias20214-362]

Portugal's [road network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_in_Portugal) includes a system of [48 motorways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highways_in_Portugal), which, as of 2023, ranked as the [seventh largest among 42 European countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_motorway_construction_in_European_nations).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Transport_and_communications_statistics_-_2024''202512-363][^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] The busiest [Portuguese airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Portugal) are [Lisbon Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon_Airport), [Porto Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_Airport), [Faro Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faro_Airport), [Madeira Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira_Airport), and [Ponta Delgada Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponta_Delgada_Airport).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Civil_aviation_yearbook_2023''202358-366] As of 2024, passenger and freight [rail transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Portugal) in Portugal operated on 2,526 km (1,570 mi) of active [railway lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_railway_lines_in_Portugal), with passenger services operated mainly by [Comboios de Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comboios_de_Portugal) (CP).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Transport_and_communications_statistics_-_2024''202511-367][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquesMoroRamos2025683–713-368] The [Port of Sines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Sines) serves as Portugal's foremost [container port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_in_Portugal) and leads the country in cargo [tonnage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonnage).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazmiRochaBrochado20255-369]

As of 2023, two-thirds of Portugal's energy supply was imported, representing the 11th-highest level of energy dependency in the European Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEObservatório_da_EnergiaDireção-Geral_de_Energia_e_Geologia:_Direção_de_Serviços_de_Planeamento_Energético_e_EstatísticaAgência_para_a_Energia:_Direção_de_Formação,_Informação_e_Educação202544–46-370] The country is pursuing [energy transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_Portugal) and has invested in the development of renewable energy sources such as the [Aguçadoura Wave Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agu%C3%A7adoura_Wave_Farm).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhatiwadaVasudevanSantos202212-371][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESilvaRusuGuedes_Soares2018-372] Portugal has expanded the role of renewable sources in its energy sector, with renewables accounting for 35.2% of energy consumption, as of 2023 up from 21.9% in 2007, and 78.1% of [total installed capacity](9540.md), compared with 59.3% in 2014.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEObservatório_da_EnergiaDireção-Geral_de_Energia_e_Geologia:_Direção_de_Serviços_de_Planeamento_Energético_e_EstatísticaAgência_para_a_Energia:_Direção_de_Formação,_Informação_e_Educação202544,_53–54,_76-373] In 2021, the country completed the phase-out of coal-fired generation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreira2025-374][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartinsMourade_Almeida202218-375]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Portugal)

See also: [Portuguese people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people)

View during the blue hour of the Luiz I Bridge and the city of Porto.

[Porto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto) is the [second largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Portugal) in Portugal, with [its metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_metropolitan_area) the country's second-largest.

In 2025, Portugal had a population of 11,424,031, of whom 9,826,492 were Portuguese nationals. The remainder were foreign residents at 1,597,539.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Estimates_of_resident_population_in_Portugal_2025''2026-8] Portugal is steadily ageing and has one of the world's highest proportion of elderly citizens, comprising nearly one-fourth of its entire population,[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_Nations_Department_of_Economic_and_Social_Affairs202323-377] together with a female share of the population at 51.1%.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Gender_equality_in_Portugal:_statistical_bulletin_2025''202516–17-379]

In 2025, Portugal had a fertility rate of 1.4, which is below the [replacement rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-replacement_fertility) of 2.1 and is one of the [world's lowest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_total_fertility_rate).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Estimates_of_resident_population_in_Portugal_2025''2026-8][^cite_note-380] Portugal has had a fertility rate below the replacement rate of 2.1 since the 1980s that has led to the country having a [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_pyramid) of 45.8, one of the [highest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_median_age).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Estimates_of_resident_population_in_Portugal_2025''2026-8][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESurkynLesthaeghe200445–86-382] As of 2025, 23.3% of the population was aged 65 or older.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Estimates_of_resident_population_in_Portugal_2025''2026-8] Despite the effects of net migration, due to low fertility rates, Portugal's population is projected to drop to 8.3 million by 2100.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Projections_of_resident_population_in_Portugal_2025-2100''2025-383]

Historically a country of emigration,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFederal_Research_Division,_Library_of_Congress1993\[httpcountrystudiesusportugal48htm_75-79\]-384] Portugal has been a net recipient of [immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Portugal) since 1993 except between 2011 and 2016 during the [Portuguese financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%932014_Portuguese_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Estimates_of_resident_population_in_Portugal_2025''2026-8][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''30_years_of_25_April:_a_statistical_portrait''20048-385][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiresVidigal20205,_6-386] Since 2016, Portugal has experienced a marked increase in immigration, with the proportion of non-nationals in the total population rising from 3.5% to 14.0% by 2025, living mainly in the Algarve and the [Lisbon metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon_metropolitan_area), and with 24.5% of all births registered in 2024 being to foreign-born women.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Survey_on_living_conditions,_origins_and_trajectories_of_the_resident_population_2023''2023-319][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388] In Portugal, White Portuguese constitute the largest [racial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_\(human_categorization\)) and [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity), representing 84.2% of the population, followed by multiracial Portuguese at 3.4%, [Black Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Portuguese) at 2.2%, [Asian Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luso-Asians) at 0.7%, and the [Romani](28870464.md) at 0.6%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Survey_on_living_conditions,_origins_and_trajectories_of_the_resident_population_2023''2023-319]

In 2023, 88% of the Portuguese population lived in urban areas.[^cite_note-389] The capital city, [Lisbon](18091.md), had a population of 658,236 as of 2025. It is part of Lisbon metropolitan area, the biggest [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_areas_in_Portugal) of Portugal with 3.4 million people.[^cite_note-population2025-390]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Portugal)

The main gothic facade of the Monastery of Batalha, Portugal

The [Batalha Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batalha_Monastery) has historically been used to transmit a religious link to Portugal's history.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELima201881–97-87]

Portugal has been a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) [since 1911](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_on_the_separation_of_church_and_state_\(Portugal\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), and it guarantees [religious freedom](46006.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Moura2024-391][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBotelho_Moniz202485–99-392][^cite_note-393] Although Portugal has no [official religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion), the [Catholic Church](563502.md) has a history there that predates the country's formation and can be traced back to the 3rd century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarneiro2009205–220-394] According to the 2021 Census, 80.2% of the Portuguese population aged 15 and older were Catholic, while 14.1% are nonreligious.[^cite_note-Censos2021-395][^cite_note-398]

The country has small [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Portugal), [Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints_in_Portugal), [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Portugal), [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Portugal), [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Portugal), [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses_in_Portugal?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Baháʼí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith_in_Portugal), [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Portugal), and [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Portugal) communities.[^cite_note-Censos2021-395] Influences from Chinese traditional religion are also evident among many people, particularly in fields related to [Traditional Chinese Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_Medicine).[^cite_note-400] Even though Portugal has deep ties with Christianity, as of 2019 the majority of its people were shown to be tolerant towards followers of other faiths, with the Muslim community perceiving itself as thoroughly integrated into Portugal and believing that the country provided conditions conducive to smooth integration.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_CarvalhoNeyroudPintoPinto20252–16-401]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Portugal)

See also: [Portuguese language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language)

Photograph of a white street sign mounted on a tan wall reads in Mirandese "Rue de L Cruzeiro" with "Rua do Cruzeiro" written in Portuguese below in script, and a small leaf logo at the top.

A sign in [Mirandese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirandese_language) in [Miranda do Douro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miranda_do_Douro)

Portuguese is the official and predominantly spoken language in Portugal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrancoGriloSilva2023195-402][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEuropean_Commission:_Directorate-General_for_Education,_Youth,_Sport_and_Culture_and_Verian202411-403] It is one of 24 official and working [languages of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-404] Portuguese is the fifth-most widely spoken first language in the world, with around 250 million native speakers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrancoGriloSilva2023195–198-405]

[Portuguese Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Sign_Language) is officially protected by the country's constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFigueira-CardosoGrzegorczyMarçalo20259–36-406] The recognised regional languages and dialects include [Mirandese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirandese_language), spoken in [Terra de Miranda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terra_de_Miranda), and [Barranquenho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barranquenho), in [Barrancos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrancos).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFigueira-CardosoGrzegorczyMarçalo20259–36-406] Portuguese people are typically bilingual, with 67.5% speaking at least two languages, and tend to be proficient non-native English speakers, with Portugal placed sixth globally for English proficiency in the 2025 [EF English Proficiency Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EF_English_Proficiency_Index).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Adult_education_survey_–_2022''2023-407][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEF_Education_First20259-408]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Portugal)

Square of the University of Coimbra

The [University of Coimbra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Coimbra) in [Coimbra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coimbra) is the first university in Portugal.

Portugal's education system is organised into [preschool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preschool) education; school education, encompassing [basic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_education_in_Portugal?action=edit\&redlink=1), [secondary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education_in_Portugal?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Portugal); and [extracurricular education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extracurricular_education_in_Portugal?action=edit\&redlink=1), which includes [continuing education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuing_education).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorreia2025316-409][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarros2025271–295-410] Optional preschool is provided for all children between three and five years old,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEuropean_Commission:_Directorate-General_for_Education,_Youth,_Sport_and_Culture202510-411][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAssembly_of_the_Republic2025-412] after which school attendance is compulsory for 12 years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabrito2023-413] Basic education lasts nine years and is divided into three cycles, while secondary education is organised into [academic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College-preparatory_school) and [vocational pathways](56551.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabrito2023-413]

Higher education is provided by universities and [polytechnic institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytechnic_\(Portugal\)) in accordance with the [Bologna Process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bologna_Process), with the [University of Lisbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Lisbon) being the country's largest university by enrolment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUrbano201195–115-414] A general requirement for higher education are the *[exames finais nacionais do ensino secundário](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exames_Nacionais_do_Ensino_Secund%C3%A1rio)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoordinators_and_authors_of_the_National_Secondary_Education_Examinations202411-415] Most secondary-school students continue their studies at public higher education institutions or the [Military Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Academy_\(Portugal\)), which also awards university degrees.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''State_of_education_2024''202579-416][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFonsecaEncarnação201231–32-417][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Main_results_of_RAIDES25_–_first_stage_–_enrolled_students_and_international_mobility_–_2025/2026''2026-418]

Although other European countries began investing in education in the 19th century, Portugal's long-established national identity, stable borders and homogeneous population contributed to a lack of political commitment to educational development until the First Republic in the early 20th century;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGomesMachado2021135–164-419] many of its reforms were subsequently reversed under the *[Estado Novo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estado_Novo_\(Portugal\))*, whose deliberate neglect of education contributed to Portugal recording Europe's highest illiteracy rate in 1964;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFelgueirasAmorim2024-420][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlvesLimaPereira201923-421] following democratisation in 1974, education became more widely accessible and underwent modernisation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECabrito2023-413]

Portugal lags behind most OECD countries on educational indicators, largely owing to the older working-age generation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''State_of_education_2024''2025117–123-422] Fewer than two-thirds of Portugal's population have completed secondary or higher education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''State_of_education_2024''2025117–123-422] Portugal's adult population aged 16 to 65 records the fifth-lowest level of knowledge and skills in the OECD, whereas school students perform at average levels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development2024a2-423][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development2024b-424][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERosaMaiaMascarenhasTeodoro202094–120-425] In 2021, the national illiteracy rate among people aged 10 or over stood at 3.1%, ranging from 1.4% to 12.3% across the country's municipalities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Census_2021_-_16th_population_census._6th_housing_census''202244–45-426] Portugal spent 4.5% of its total GDP on education in 2022.[^cite_note-427]

### Health

Main articles: [Health in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Portugal) and [Healthcare in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Portugal)

The Coimbra University Hospital, part of the Centro Hospitalar e Universitário de Coimbra, in Coimbra.

The [Centro Hospitalar e Universitário de Coimbra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Hospitalar_e_Universit%C3%A1rio_de_Coimbra) in [Coimbra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coimbra) is the largest medical complex in Portugal.

[^cite_note-428]

Portugal has a predominantly [universal tax-funded health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) system, called *[Serviço Nacional de Saúde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Service_\(Portugal\))* (SNS), that operates alongside public-sector health insurance schemes called health subsystems funded mainly through employee and employer contributions, as well as private voluntary health insurance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFronteiraAugustoMaresso20252-429] Primary and hospital care are provided by both public and private entities, with the private sector and complementing services offered by the SNS.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFronteiraAugustoMaresso20253-430] In 2023, Portugal had the 3rd lowest public share on health spending in the European Union and over half of public hospitals in [technical insolvency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insolvency) in 2020.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_DevelopmentEuropean_Observatory_on_Health_Systems_and_Policies20257-431][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDubas-JakóbczykNdayishimiyeSzetelaSowada20253-432] Portugal has a high level of health inequity in the OECD, driven by limited public spending, uneven distribution of services, low household income, long waiting times, and policy shortcomings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFernandesFurtadoPereira2024104–111-433]

Portugal [ranked 23rd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) in the world in 2024 in life expectancy with 85.4 years for women and 79.8 years for men, and it had an [infant mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality_rate) of 2.7 deaths per 1,000 [live births](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Live_birth_\(human\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_DevelopmentEuropean_Observatory_on_Health_Systems_and_Policies20252-434][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_Nations_Inter-Agency_Group_for_Child_Mortality_Estimation202694-435] In 2022, the leading cause of death were cardiovascular diseases, at 25.4%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_DevelopmentEuropean_Observatory_on_Health_Systems_and_Policies20252-434] Portugal presents several adverse public-health indicators, including the 3rd lowest share of people reporting good health in the European Union, in 2024, at 54%, the highest alcohol consumption with 11.9 litres (2.6 imp gal; 3.1 US gal) consumed per person, in 2020, and the highest share of adult population living a [sedentary lifestyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedentary_lifestyle), in 2022, at 73%, a pattern reinforced by limited active-transport infrastructure and unsafe conditions for pedestrians and cyclists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEuropean_Commission:_Directorate-General_for_Education,_Youth,_Sport_and_Culture20229-436][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrganisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_DevelopmentEuropean_Observatory_on_Health_Systems_and_Policies20252–3,_5–6-437] The percentage of the Portuguese population suffering from moderate or severe [food insecurity](216361.md) between 2022 and 2024 was on average 11.9%, which makes Portugal the third-highest country in [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe) for this indicator where the average stands at 5.9%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_state_of_food_security_and_nutrition_in_the_world_2025_–_addressing_high_food_price_inflation_for_food_security_and_nutrition''2025120-438] Stakeholders have raised concerns over the effectiveness of food safety policies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERodriguesSaraivaGarcía-DíezCastro20255–7,_12–13-439]

## Culture

Main articles: [Culture of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Portugal) and [Mass media in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Portugal)

Women wearing red traditional Portuguese costumes with embroidered aprons and gold jewellery at the Romaria de Nossa Senhora da Agonia.

The women of the [North Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Region,_Portugal) practice the traditional art of [gold-thread embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viana_gold?action=edit\&redlink=1), as seen in this photo of [Romaria de Nossa Senhora da Agonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romaria_de_Nossa_Senhora_da_Agonia?action=edit\&redlink=1) in [Viana do Castelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viana_do_Castelo).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaestrelloFavaro2022261–264-440]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEdos_Santos_Mota2016175–188-441]

Portugal's culture has developed due to the influence from various civilisations that have crossed Europe, especially the Mediterranean.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESuisse202642–59-442][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArruda2009124-443] Later, during the period of [Portugal's engagement in the Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal_\(1415%E2%80%931578\)), cultural elements from outside of Europe were introduced and became a central element of Portuguese national identity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECasimiro20201–17-444][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeralta2026341–364-445]

Portugal is known for [its heritage and architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_architecture),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGholitabarAlipourCosta20182-446] [sacred sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_of_F%C3%A1tima),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeidariYazdaniSaghafiJalilvand2018751-447] [festivities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_festivals_in_Portugal),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhoCosta202273–82-448][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharzyńskiŁyszkiewiczMusiał201795–96-449] [poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_poetry),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELadeira200843–72-450] its music (especially the [fado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fado)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhoMadeiraMonteiroLopes20232-451] and [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_cuisine) (including [its wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_wine)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECosta_e_SilvaElo2017425–442-452][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharzyńskiŁyszkiewiczMusiał201785–100-453] As of 2026, [UNESCO](21786641.md) inscribed [17 properties in Portugal on the World Heritage List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Portugal).[^cite_note-454] Portugal's [national day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_day) is on 10 June, celebrated as the *Dia de Portugal, de Camões e das Comunidades Portuguesas* ([Portugal, Camões, and Portuguese Communities Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_Day)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJoão201119–34-455]

### Art and architecture

Main articles: [Portuguese art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_art) and [Architecture of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Portugal)

Six paintings attributed to Nuno Gonçalves presenting a 58 people of the Court and various groups of Portuguese society at that time, gathered together around the double figuration of St. Vincent with expressive look of concentration on their faces, engaged in an act of veneration.

The *[Saint Vincent Panels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Vincent_Panels)* (c. 1470) by [Nuno Gonçalves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuno_Gon%C3%A7alves) are one of the most significant works of Portuguese painting from the 15th century.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermeerschRousakiLyckeValadas20251–2-456]

The history of visual art in Portugal dates back into the [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_the_Upper_Paleolithic). An early evidence showing an attempt at depicting motion was found at the [Prehistoric Rock Art Sites in the Côa Valley and Siega Verde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Rock_Art_Sites_in_the_C%C3%B4a_Valley_and_Siega_Verde).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Evaluation_report_of_cultural_properties_-_1998''199846-457] Over time, foreign and native influences, together with advancements in manufacturing, developed distinctive art styles, such as the [Manueline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manueline) and [Pombaline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pombaline_style),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECâmara1999197–214-458] and also a number of crafts that are typical of Portugal, namely the *[azulejo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azulejo)*, *[talha dourada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilded_woodcarving_in_Portugal)*, and [Portuguese pavement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_pavement).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanduMurtaNevesPereira201647–71-459][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEResende2022206–227-460][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPereiraConteEstevesPais20233804–3815-461]

Historically, Christianity played an influential role in Portuguese art and was a recurrent theme in many art forms, including painting.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermeerschRousakiLyckeValadas20251–2-456][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESerrão200653–57,_68-462] Throughout the country's history, artwork in Portugal was typically done by local artists who, depending on location, followed different variations in style, giving Portugal a diverse array of artistic styles throughout the country, an example of this being the [thatch houses of Santana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typical_thatch_houses_of_Santana?action=edit\&redlink=1), in Madeira.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESilvada_Silva2024-463]

### Literature

Main article: [Portuguese literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_literature)

Drawing of Luís Vaz de Camões

[Luís Vaz de Camões](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_Vaz_de_Cam%C3%B5es) was the writer of *[Os Lusíadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Os_Lus%C3%ADadas)*.

Portugal has a literary tradition that predates the Portuguese language going back into the early 13th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohenParkinson200937–43-464] Portuguese literature developed through song as well as the written page known as *[cantigas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantigas)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMonteagudo2007275–312-465] The *cantigas* drew practitioners from all social ranging from King [Denis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denis_of_Portugal) to [Martin Codax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Codax) who was a [minstrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minstrel).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacedo20092-466] The earliest known work of literature produced by a Portuguese is the *[Ora faz ost'o senhor de Navarra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ora_faz_ost'o_senhor_de_Navarra)*, a *[cantiga de escárnio e maldizer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantigas_de_esc%C3%A1rnio_e_maldizer)* written in [Galician–Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician%E2%80%93Portuguese) by [João Soares de Paiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Soares_de_Paiva) at around the year 1200.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastro2015120-467][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiranda20122-468]

Portuguese literature developed under the influence of both European geopolitical developments and broader European literary traditions. The [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years'_War) helped foster the development of Portuguese [chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicle) by [Fernão Lopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fern%C3%A3o_Lopes), which constitute a valuable record of some of Europe's early encounters with peoples beyond the continent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacedo20092–5-469][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParkinson200945–51-470] European medieval chivalric literature, together with didactic religious literature transmitted through adaptations and partial translations, contributed to the development of Portuguese poetry in the translated works of [Norman French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_French) [Arturian narratives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arthurian_literature).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacedo20092-466][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParkinson200953–54-471] Portuguese literature flourished during the Age of Discovery with writers such as [Luís Vaz de Camões](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_Vaz_de_Cam%C3%B5es) and [António Ferreira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_Ferreira_\(poet\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis200972–83-472][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerkinsEarle200968-473] Modern Portuguese literature took shape through the work of [Almeida Garrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almeida_Garrett), one of the founders of Portuguese Romanticism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhão_Buescu2009109–111-474] Portugal has one [Nobel Prize–winning author](23385442.md)—[José Saramago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Saramago) (1998).[^cite_note-475]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Portugal)

Black-and-white photograph of Amália Rodrigues singing into a standing microphone, wearing a long dark gown and shawl, with one hand raised in expression and a guitarist partly visible in the background.

[Amália Rodrigues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Am%C3%A1lia_Rodrigues) was known as the "Queen of Fado".

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAguiar-Branco20241-476]

The [recorded history of music in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_history_of_Portugal) dates back to the 6th century. The earliest documentation of a church singer in Portugal is from the year 525 and refers to an individual named André. Portuguese music initially consisted mostly of [liturgical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_music) and [troubadourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician-Portuguese_lyric).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerreira200810-477][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerreira2014170–178-478] Over time, new folk traditions together with the influence of foreign cultures and the creation of [new instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Portuguese_musical_instruments), especially [guitars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_guitars), led to a diverse variety of regional folk music such as the fado, the [Coimbra fado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coimbra_fado), and [Madeira's folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Madeira).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERibeiro20261–18-479][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELãSilvaGranqvist2023631.e7-480][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEdos_Santos2013147–153-481]

[Popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Portuguese_musicians) in Portugal after the Carnation Revolution has been heavily influenced by American trends, which has led into the evolution of [hip-hop tuga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop_tuga) and popularisation of [rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_music_in_Portugal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlge20137–24-482] Historically, Portugal has been a country of [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) which has heavily influenced the [pimba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pimba) in the 20th century and led to the introduction of Portuguese music into other cultures such as the [ukelele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukelele) in [Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Hawaii) in the 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElliott2010\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidSa-gR-P-pVECqpimbamusicpgPA135_135\]-483][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENidel2004312-484]

### Holidays and festivities

Main article: [Public holidays in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Portugal)

People dressed up as Caretos leap around a bonfire during the Entrudo.

Two [Caretos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Careto) at the [Entrudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrudo?action=edit\&redlink=1), in [Vinhais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinhais)

Officially, Portugal has 13 national, government-recognised holidays.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorgal20261–13-485] Public holidays in Portugal are regulated by the [Labour Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Labour_Code?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorgal20261–13-485] Besides the national holidays, there are regional holidays celebrated only in Madeira and the Azores and two facultative holidays which are the [Entrudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrudo?action=edit\&redlink=1) and one municipal holiday allowed per [concelho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concelho).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAssembly_of_the_Republic2009-486] Unlike some official holidays, Portugal's folk festivities remain widely attended and are rooted in local traditions, such as the [Carnival of Ovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival_of_Ovar), Lisbon's [Marchas Populares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marchas_Populares), and Porto's [Festa de São João](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festa_de_S%C3%A3o_Jo%C3%A3o_do_Porto).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorgal20261–13-485][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhoCosta202273-487] Many of folk festivities are associated with the *[Santos Populares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_saint)*, Christian celebrations with older pagan origins linked to [Midsummer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midsummer).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhoCosta202274–78-488] These festivities are celebrated across the country, especially in rural areas, and typically include music performances, stages, temporary arenas, and traditional food stalls.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhoCosta202279–82-489]

### Cuisine

Main articles: [Portuguese cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_cuisine) and [Portuguese wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_wine)

Photograph of a bakery display case filled with pastéis de nata, with a sign in front labelled "Pastel de Nata" and describing them as a low-sugar secret recipe in Portuguese and English.

*[Pastéis de nata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastel_de_nata)*

Portuguese cuisine is influenced by both the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_diet) and [Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_diet) diets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuinéFlorençaAmaralCosta20251–2-490] Seafood, *[brassicas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brassicas)*, potatoes, bread, dairy, and [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) are traditional staples.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELorenzoIzquierdoRodriguez-CarneroFernández-Pombo20221725–1747-491] *[Bacalhau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacalhau)* has such a broad presence in Portugal that it is considered a [national dish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_dish), along with the *[pastel de nata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastel_de_nata)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESáda_Silva20194–19-492][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECosta_e_SilvaElo2017425–442-452] Traditional Portuguese sweets are known as [conventual sweets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conventual_sweets). Large quantities of sugar and eggs are used.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarata_Dias2018275–299-493]

Popular Portuguese beverages include its wines, a craft that is well establish since the 7th century, and of which are such examples as [Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_wine) and [Madeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira_wine).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECunhaIbáñezTeixeira-SantosBrazão2020-494][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharzyńskiŁyszkiewiczMusiał201785–100-453] [Beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_Portugal) has been brewed in Portugal since the [Chalcolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcolithic_Europe).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEstreicher202540-495] Tea has been produced on São Miguel Island since the 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoura2019112–130-496]

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Portugal)



[Cristiano Ronaldo](623737.md) is widely considered one of the [greatest football players in history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_career_achievements_by_Cristiano_Ronaldo).&#x20;

Portugal has contributed to the popularity and globalisation of sport through internationally recognised athletes such as [Cristiano Ronaldo](623737.md), [Ricardinho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardinho_\(futsal_player,_born_1985\)), [Naide Gomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naide_Gomes), and [Carlos Lopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Lopes), as well as through the [popularity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sports_clubs_by_membership) of its three largest [football clubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Three_\(Portugal\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcía-FernándezGrimaldi-PuyanaBravo20222-497][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarvalhoGarcía-CiracSousa202236-498] [Football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Portugal) is the most popular sport in Portugal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlmeida202275–88-499] The [Portugal men's national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_men's_national_football_team) won the [UEFA European Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_European_Championship) in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2016) and the [UEFA Nations League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Nations_League) in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018%E2%80%9319_UEFA_Nations_League) and [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%9325_UEFA_Nations_League).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Full_time_report_final_-_Sunday_10_July_2016_Stade_de_France_-_Saint-Denis''2016-500][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Full_time_report_final_-_Sunday_9_June_2019_-_Estádio_do_Dragão_-_Porto''2019-501][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Full_time_report_final_-_Sunday_8_June_2025_-_Munich_Football_Arena''2025-502]

Portugal is among the world's leading [futsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futsal_in_Portugal) countries,[^cite_note-503] with its [men's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_national_futsal_team) having won the [FIFA Futsal World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_Futsal_World_Cup) in [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_FIFA_Futsal_World_Cup),[^cite_note-504] the [UEFA Futsal Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Futsal_Championship) in [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Futsal_Euro_2018) and [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Futsal_Euro_2022),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''UEFA_Futsal_EURO_2018_tournament_review''201823-505][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''UEFA_Futsal_EURO_2022_tournament_review''202227-506] and the [Futsal Finalissima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futsal_Finalissima) in [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Futsal_Finalissima).[^cite_note-507] In [athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_of_athletics_in_Portugal?action=edit\&redlink=1), the country has set several [records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Portuguese_records_in_athletics) and has earned more medals in this sport than in any other at both the [Olympic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_at_the_Olympics) and [Paralympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_at_the_Paralympics).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''2024_activities_and_accounts_report''202554–95-508][^cite_note-509][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Paralympic_medals''20262–9-510] In traditional sport, Portugal is noted for its [classical dressage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_School_of_Equestrian_Art) as well as native sports such as [jogo do pau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jogo_do_pau) and [jogo da malha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jogo_da_malha?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPickel-Chevalier202022–44-511][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERamírez-MacíasRodríguez-SánchezLasaga-Rodríguez2022199–208-512] Portugal has several established sporting centres across the country, including the Algarve and Lisbon, which are international golf destinations and have hosted motorsport events such as [Formula One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Grand_Prix) and [Grand Prix motorcycle racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_motorcycle_Grand_Prix),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoreira2018-513][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastroMenezes202529–47-514][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMourãoMacedoSánchez-Carreira2025198-515] as well as [Nazaré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praia_do_Norte_\(Nazar%C3%A9\)) and [Peniche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supertubos), which are known for surfing and annually host the [TUDOR Nazaré Big Wave Challenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TUDOR_Nazar%C3%A9_Big_Wave_Challenge?action=edit\&redlink=1) and the [MEO Rip Curl Pro Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MEO_Rip_Curl_Pro_Portugal), respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFernandesRibeiroFerreira2017703–712-516][^cite_note-517][^cite_note-518]

[^cite_note-2]: &#x20;The modern day concept of sovereignty or [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_independence) did not exist at the time, nor was established any notion of its recognition. Portugal was recognised as a kingdom with its own King in [1179](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifestis_Probatum) by the [Pope](23056.md) who was the ultimate authority in Europe at the time. The way Europe was seen at the time was as a *[Res publica christiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Res_publica_christiana)*.<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERistuccia201815–19-1)</sup>

[^cite_note-6]: &#x20;Portugal's land area covers 92,225 km<sup>2</sup> (35,608 mi<sup>2</sup>),<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamosRamos-Pereira20204-3)</sup> while the territory over which it exercises sovereignty—including land, [internal waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_waters), and the [territorial sea](496983.md#Territorial_sea)—amounts to 156,597 km<sup>2</sup> (60,462 mi<sup>2</sup>).<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerrão2016123–134-4)</sup><sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBessa_Pacheco2014112–116-5)</sup>

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;Portuguese: *República Portuguesa*, IPA: [\[rɛˈpuβlikɐ puɾtuˈɣez̻ɐ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Portuguese)

[^cite_note-22]: &#x20;The earliest human trace found in Portugal is the 400,000-year-old [Aroeira 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aroeira_3), a *[Homo heidelbergensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_heidelbergensis)* skull discovered in the [Cave of Aroeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_of_Aroeira) in 2014.<sup>[\[17\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDauraSanzArsuagaHoffmann20173397–3399,_3401-21)</sup>

[^cite_note-65]: &#x20;There are five surviving 12th-century documents that refer to the Battle of Ourique, none of which mention any apparition of Christ to D. Afonso Henriques, the earliest such references appear in the 15th century. According to the historian Rodrigo Franco da Costa: "No document until the 15th century mentions the apparition of Christ to D. Afonso Henriques on the eve of the Battle of Ourique. Those that have been presented are not conclusive. There are, however, 'five' 12th century documents that refer to the battle — yet none of them mention the apparition, even though some narrate miracles. The first certain references to the miracle appear in the mid-15th century. Once it emerged, the legend gradually expanded until it acquired its definitive form in the writers of the sixteenth century ... We therefore have, on one side, the silence of the three centuries closest to the battle concerning the miracle of the apparition, and on the other, the late appearance of the legend under circumstances that point to an evolutionary process. It should also be noted that, according to the 16th century version, Christ did not merely appear and promise victory — He also prophesied the future destinies of Portugal. Thus, the contemporaries of the conquering king supposedly received from God Himself the titles legitimizing the new monarchy and the guarantee of its glorious future. Yet it is precisely this fact of the divine foundation of Portugal — the most important fact of all for the Portuguese — about which everyone remains silent, while they do speak of the battle that supposedly occurred."<sup>[\[59\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFranco_da_Costa2014-64)</sup>

[^cite_note-69]: &#x20;Unlike popular belief, there are no surviving documents indicating that a treaty was signed at Zamora in 1143 recognising Portugal's status as a kingdom by the [Kingdom of León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Le%C3%B3n). Historian Bernard F. Reilly states that "In October he invited Afonso Henriques to a conference at Zamora. The Leonese monarch wished not only to secure his own border with Portugal in the coming months but also to obtain the collaboration of his cousin in simultaneous attacks on the Almoravids and he was willing to make the greatest concessions to achieve such cooperation. The evidence for what transpired between the two at Zamora is certainly less than we should wish. In the first instance, it consists in two royal charters, one to a Martin Cidez and another to Pons de Cabrera (D458-59). Although the Portuguese ruler does not appear as a confirmant of either he is cited in the dating formula of both. They speak of the Council of Valladolid celebrated by Cardinal Guido and of the 'king of Portugal' who came to hold a conference with the emperor. Since the charters were drafted by Alfonso VII's chancery, the recognition of the new royal status of Afonso Henriques is implicit."<sup>[\[61\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReilly1998\[httpsbooksgoogleptbooksidAVIrEAAAQBAJpgPA80_80-81]-67)</sup>

[^cite_note-96]: &#x20;The concept of discovery that applies to Portuguese overseas activities in the 15th and 16th centuries emerged in the [Treaty of Alcáçovas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Alc%C3%A1%C3%A7ovas). After the 1480s it became the main criterion for territorial claims. Before then, Portuguese expansion centred on conquest, [religious war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_war), and [forced conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_the_Age_of_Discovery). Prior to the treaty, documents made no reference to discovery.<sup>[\[88\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMenez202551,_59,_60–61,_73-95)</sup>

[^cite_note-102]: &#x20;Portugal's colonial empire endured from the [conquest of Ceuta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_conquest_of_Ceuta) in 1415 to the [handover of Macau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handover_of_Macau) in 1999. Historian L. Veracini states that Portugal was the first and the last explicitly colonial power.<sup>[\[91\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEL'Estoile2023xii-99)</sup><sup>[\[92\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVeracini202312-100)</sup><sup>[\[93\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchriffl2022487-101)</sup>

[^cite_note-194]: &#x20;The territory of Portugal includes its land, [internal waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_waters), and [territorial sea](496983.md#Territorial_sea).<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerrão2016123–134-4)</sup>

[^cite_note-200]: &#x20;The highest peak in [mainland Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Portugal) is [Torre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre_\(Serra_da_Estrela\)) in [Serra da Estrela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serra_da_Estrela), at an elevation of 1,993 metres (6,539 ft).<sup>[\[189\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndradeMouratoRamos2021-199)</sup>

[^cite_note-258]: &#x20;The president also holds exceptional powers besides the moderating powers, which are not exclusive to the office.<sup>[\[246\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGomesAlbergariaCanotilho2021514-257)</sup>

[^cite_note-324]: &#x20;Such as constrained resources, inadequate conditions, insufficient transparency, and the risk of non-compliance with [international human rights standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoners'_rights_in_international_law).<sup>[\[297\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELança_Pereira2026181–197-309)</sup>

[^cite_note-393]: &#x20;The [1976 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Portugal) guaranteed [religious freedom](46006.md) without distinction between denominations and without any specific limits, breaking with earlier regimes of union, secularist neutrality, and preferential relations with the Catholic Church. However, the legal instruments of 1940 were affected in 1975, when it was introduced the dissolubility of Catholic marriage, and remained in force until 2004, when the government and the [Holy See](13393.md) agreed a new Concordat.<sup>[\[379\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBotelho_Moniz202485–99-392)</sup>

[^cite_note-398]: &#x20;Although Portugal is [overwhelmingly Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_by_country), the majority are [non-practicing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Christians), a discrepancy that might have existed throughout its religious history.<sup>[\[381\]](#cite_note-Censos2021-395)</sup><sup>[\[382\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoutinho20231–15-396)</sup><sup>[\[383\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDix202520-397)</sup>

[^cite_note-400]: &#x20;Chinese medical science is well known in Portugal and is widely accepted by the people. Portugal is the first European Union country that specifically provided legislation for acupuncture and Chinese medical science.<sup>[\[384\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESongGaoJia2021-399)</sup>
